Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, thank you for seeing me. I... I came in because, well, I just can't seem to stop worrying. It's like, once a worry starts, it just takes over completely. For example, my mother has been getting up a lot in the middle of the night lately, and I just lie awake thinking about her falling, or what if she leaves the stove on, even though she hasn't done that in years. And then, at work—I'm a librarian, and I handle a lot of our special collections and grant reports—if I have a report due, I obsess over every little detail. Did I miss a citation? Did I format the budget right? I make lists, you know, endless lists, trying to organize my thoughts and put them away, but I just can't seem to shelve the worries in my mind. I try to redirect myself, maybe read a book or watch a show, but the thoughts just keep looping and looping, no matter how hard I try to stop them. It's just exhausting, honestly, to constantly feel like I'm missing something or that something terrible is going to happen because I wasn't paying enough attention.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Rituals? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I know what you mean, like having to touch a doorknob a certain number of times or wash your hands over and over. I've read about things like that—we actually have a really robust psychology and behavioral sciences section in the library, and I process a lot of the new acquisitions, so I see those topics come across my desk quite a bit. But no, I don't do anything like that. I don't have a specific physical action or a routine I feel forced to do. 

It's really more just... the lists I mentioned earlier. But I wouldn't call making those lists a ritual, exactly. It's more of a desperate attempt to just get the swirling thoughts out of my head and onto a piece of paper so maybe I can look at them objectively and organize them, the way I'd organize a catalog or a finding aid for an archive. But the thing is, it doesn't even work to reduce the worry! I'll write down "check mother's medication schedule" or "verify the budget formatting on the humanities grant proposal," and instead of feeling relieved that it's written down and handled, I just stare at the paper and start worrying that I forgot to add something else to the list, or that I might accidentally throw the list away. 

So, no, I don't have any behaviors that actually make the worry go away or that I feel compelled to perform in that strict sense. I honestly wish I did have something that worked to just turn it off. Sometimes I think it would be so much easier if I could just arrange the pens on my desk in a specific way and suddenly feel calm, but no, it doesn't work like that for me. It's strictly in my head. It's just this constant, endless mental loop of "what if, what if, what if" that I can't seem to stop with any sort of physical action, no matter how hard I try to just sit still and reason with myself.
=====================================

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F42\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, thank you for seeing me. I... I came in because, well, I just can't seem to stop worrying. It's like, once a worry starts, it just takes over completely. For example, my mother has been getting up a lot in the middle of the night lately, and I just lie awake thinking about her falling, or what if she leaves the stove on, even though she hasn't done that in years. And then, at work—I'm a librarian, and I handle a lot of our special collections and grant reports—if I have a report due, I obsess over every little detail. Did I miss a citation? Did I format the budget right? I make lists, you know, endless lists, trying to organize my thoughts and put them away, but I just can't seem to shelve the worries in my mind. I try to redirect myself, maybe read a book or watch a show, but the thoughts just keep looping and looping, no matter how hard I try to stop them. It's just exhausting, honestly, to constantly feel like I'm missing something or that something terrible is going to happen because I wasn't paying enough attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your worries?\n\nPatient: Rituals? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I know what you mean, like having to touch a doorknob a certain number of times or wash your hands over and over. I've read about things like that—we actually have a really robust psychology and behavioral sciences section in the library, and I process a lot of the new acquisitions, so I see those topics come across my desk quite a bit. But no, I don't do anything like that. I don't have a specific physical action or a routine I feel forced to do. \n\nIt's really more just... the lists I mentioned earlier. But I wouldn't call making those lists a ritual, exactly. It's more of a desperate attempt to just get the swirling thoughts out of my head and onto a piece of paper so maybe I can look at them objectively and organize them, the way I'd organize a catalog or a finding aid for an archive. But the thing is, it doesn't even work to reduce the worry! I'll write down \"check mother's medication schedule\" or \"verify the budget formatting on the humanities grant proposal,\" and instead of feeling relieved that it's written down and handled, I just stare at the paper and start worrying that I forgot to add something else to the list, or that I might accidentally throw the list away. \n\nSo, no, I don't have any behaviors that actually make the worry go away or that I feel compelled to perform in that strict sense. I honestly wish I did have something that worked to just turn it off. Sometimes I think it would be so much easier if I could just arrange the pens on my desk in a specific way and suddenly feel calm, but no, it doesn't work like that for me. It's strictly in my head. It's just this constant, endless mental loop of \"what if, what if, what if\" that I can't seem to stop with any sort of physical action, no matter how hard I try to just sit still and reason with myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, intrusive thoughts? I guess it depends on what exactly you mean by intrusive. If you mean those really shocking or bizarre thoughts that just pop into your head out of nowhere—like the ones I have read about in some of the abnormal psychology journals we archive, where people imagine doing something terrible or completely out of character or just having these bizarre images flash in their minds—then no, I do not have anything like that at all. Nothing that feels alien or completely disconnected from who I am or what I care about. 

But if you just mean thoughts that keep coming back and that I desperately want to go away, well, that really just goes back to the worries I was just talking about. They definitely recur. I mean, they are constantly recurring, all day long. And I absolutely try to ignore them or push them away. Like when I am trying to relax on a Sunday evening and suddenly I start obsessing over whether I properly secured the climate-controlled display case for the 18th-century manuscripts we just put out on the second floor. I try so hard to suppress it, I tell myself to just watch my television show and that the case is locked and the alarm is set, but the thought just keeps tapping me on the shoulder, over and over. 

But it is not a weird or random thought, you see? It is directly related to my actual responsibilities as a librarian. It is just that the volume is turned up so high and it just will not leave me alone. So I do not think I have what you would call intrusive thoughts in that specific sense, just... very loud, very persistent everyday worries that I cannot seem to shut off no matter how much I try to distract myself or reason with my own brain.

I try so hard to suppress it, I tell myself to just watch my television show and that the case is locked and the alarm is set, but the thought just keeps tapping me on the shoulder, over and over. \n\nBut it is not a weird or random thought, you see? It is directly related to my actual responsibilities as a librarian. It is just that the volume is turned up so high and it just will not leave me alone. So I do not think I have what you would call intrusive thoughts in that specific sense, just... very loud, very persistent everyday worries that I cannot seem to shut off no matter how much I try to distract myself or reason with my own brain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I'll write down \"check mother's medication schedule\" or \"verify the budget formatting on the humanities grant proposal,\" and instead of feeling relieved that it's written down and handled, I just stare at the paper and start worrying that I forgot to add something else to the list, or that I might accidentally throw the list away. \n\nSo, no, I don't have any behaviors that actually make the worry go away or that I feel compelled to perform in that strict sense. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, check things over and over... no, not really. I mean, I check things, of course. As a librarian handling special collections, checking is just part of the job. When I set up a display case, I check the lock, I check the humidity monitor, I make sure the alarm sensor is active. That's just standard protocol. But I don't go back and check it five times, or feel some uncontrollable urge to keep testing the handle until my hand hurts, or anything like that. 

Once I do it, I know I've done it. The physical act of checking is finished. It's just that later, when I'm at home, my mind starts telling me, "Are you *sure* you checked it? What if you just thought you checked it, but you actually didn't turn the key all the way?" So I'll sit there on my couch, agonizing over it, picturing the lock in my mind, trying to retrace my steps to prove to myself that I did it right. But I don't actually get up, drive back to the library, and physically check the case again. I just suffer through the worry in my head. 

And it's the same with my mother. I'll make sure her pill organizer is filled on Sunday afternoon. I do it carefully, I double-check the bottles against her chart, and I snap the little lids shut. I know it's done. But then on Wednesday night, I'll be lying in bed, wide awake, thinking, "Did I put the blood pressure pill in the Tuesday slot or the Wednesday slot? What if she took two today? What if she misses a dose and has a stroke?" I'll just lay there spinning out these awful scenarios. But I don't actually get out of bed, drive over to her house in the middle of the night, and count her pills. I just... worry about it. Endlessly. So the checking isn't the problem, it's the worrying about whether my checking was good enough that just completely exhausts me.

It's just this constant, endless mental loop of \"what if, what if, what if\" that I can't seem to stop with any sort of physical action, no matter how hard I try to just sit still and reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Um, intrusive thoughts? I guess it depends on what exactly you mean by intrusive. If you mean those really shocking or bizarre thoughts that just pop into your head out of nowhere—like the ones I have read about in some of the abnormal psychology journals we archive, where people imagine doing something terrible or completely out of character or just having these bizarre images flash in their minds—then no, I do not have anything like that at all. Nothing that feels alien or completely disconnected from who I am or what I care about. \n\nBut if you just mean thoughts that keep coming back and that I desperately want to go away, well, that really just goes back to the worries I was just talking about. They definitely recur. I mean, they are constantly recurring, all day long. And I absolutely try to ignore them or push them away. Like when I am trying to relax on a Sunday evening and suddenly I start obsessing over whether I properly secured the climate-controlled display case for the 18th-century manuscripts we just put out on the second floor. I try so hard to suppress it, I tell myself to just watch my television show and that the case is locked and the alarm is set, but the thought just keeps tapping me on the shoulder, over and over. \n\nBut it is not a weird or random thought, you see? It is directly related to my actual responsibilities as a librarian. It is just that the volume is turned up so high and it just will not leave me alone. So I do not think I have what you would call intrusive thoughts in that specific sense, just... very loud, very persistent everyday worries that I cannot seem to shut off no matter how much I try to distract myself or reason with my own brain.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a strong need to check things over and over, like your mother's medication schedule or the library's display cases?\n\nPatient: Well, check things over and over... no, not really. I mean, I check things, of course. As a librarian handling special collections, checking is just part of the job. When I set up a display case, I check the lock, I check the humidity monitor, I make sure the alarm sensor is active. That's just standard protocol. But I don't go back and check it five times, or feel some uncontrollable urge to keep testing the handle until my hand hurts, or anything like that. \n\nOnce I do it, I know I've done it. The physical act of checking is finished. It's just that later, when I'm at home, my mind starts telling me, \"Are you *sure* you checked it? What if you just thought you checked it, but you actually didn't turn the key all the way?\" So I'll sit there on my couch, agonizing over it, picturing the lock in my mind, trying to retrace my steps to prove to myself that I did it right. But I don't actually get up, drive back to the library, and physically check the case again. I just suffer through the worry in my head. \n\nAnd it's the same with my mother. I'll make sure her pill organizer is filled on Sunday afternoon. I do it carefully, I double-check the bottles against her chart, and I snap the little lids shut. I know it's done. But then on Wednesday night, I'll be lying in bed, wide awake, thinking, \"Did I put the blood pressure pill in the Tuesday slot or the Wednesday slot? What if she took two today? What if she misses a dose and has a stroke?\" I'll just lay there spinning out these awful scenarios. But I don't actually get out of bed, drive over to her house in the middle of the night, and count her pills. I just... worry about it. Endlessly. So the checking isn't the problem, it's the worrying about whether my checking was good enough that just completely exhausts me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, yes, exactly. They are very much about everyday things, but it’s like... it’s like they just take over everything. It used to be mostly just about my mother, you know? Just worrying if she was safe, if her health was okay, if she was taking her medication like I mentioned. That was always a background hum. But over this past year, it’s like the worry has just... spread. It’s metastasized, for lack of a better word. 

Now it’s not just her. It’s my finances—I’ll sit there wondering if I’m putting enough into my retirement account, or if the roof on the house is going to need replacing soon and how I’ll afford it. And then it bleeds into my work at the library. I’ll agonize over staff decisions, like if I scheduled the student workers properly or if I was too harsh when I corrected someone about the cataloging system. And the deadlines! We have these grant deadlines for preservation projects, and even if we are weeks ahead of schedule, I just feel this knot in my stomach that we’re going to miss it, that the funding will fall through, and it will be entirely my fault. 

And then it comes back to the house, like I said, or just... am I being a good daughter? Am I doing enough for my mother, or am I just going through the motions? It’s exhausting. Even the smallest things, like a slightly weird noise from my car engine on the way to work, or an email from the library director that just says "see me later"—things that most people would just brush off—they suddenly feel like this massive thread that, if I pull it, my whole life is just going to completely unravel. So, yes, they are everyday things, absolutely, but the sheer volume of them, and how heavy they feel... it’s just overwhelming.

I'll make sure her pill organizer is filled on Sunday afternoon. I do it carefully, I double-check the bottles against her chart, and I snap the little lids shut. I know it's done. But then on Wednesday night, I'll be lying in bed, wide awake, thinking, \"Did I put the blood pressure pill in the Tuesday slot or the Wednesday slot? What if she took two today? What if she misses a dose and has a stroke?\" I'll just lay there spinning out these awful scenarios. But I don't actually get out of bed, drive over to her house in the middle of the night, and count her pills. I just... worry about it. Endlessly. So the checking isn't the problem, it's the worrying about whether my checking was good enough that just completely exhausts me.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about everyday things that could potentially happen to you or someone you care about?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes, exactly. They are very much about everyday things, but it’s like... it’s like they just take over everything. It used to be mostly just about my mother, you know? Just worrying if she was safe, if her health was okay, if she was taking her medication like I mentioned. That was always a background hum. But over this past year, it’s like the worry has just... spread. It’s metastasized, for lack of a better word. \n\nNow it’s not just her. It’s my finances—I’ll sit there wondering if I’m putting enough into my retirement account, or if the roof on the house is going to need replacing soon and how I’ll afford it. And then it bleeds into my work at the library. I’ll agonize over staff decisions, like if I scheduled the student workers properly or if I was too harsh when I corrected someone about the cataloging system. And the deadlines! We have these grant deadlines for preservation projects, and even if we are weeks ahead of schedule, I just feel this knot in my stomach that we’re going to miss it, that the funding will fall through, and it will be entirely my fault. \n\nAnd then it comes back to the house, like I said, or just... am I being a good daughter? Am I doing enough for my mother, or am I just going through the motions? It’s exhausting. Even the smallest things, like a slightly weird noise from my car engine on the way to work, or an email from the library director that just says \"see me later\"—things that most people would just brush off—they suddenly feel like this massive thread that, if I pull it, my whole life is just going to completely unravel. So, yes, they are everyday things, absolutely, but the sheer volume of them, and how heavy they feel... it’s just overwhelming.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes... yes, they really are. And that’s honestly one of the things that’s scaring me the most right now, because I have always prided myself on being very competent, very detail-oriented. You have to be, in special collections. But lately... well, I’ve started missing things. Small things, mostly, but important details. Like, just last week, I was reviewing the provenance records for a new acquisition, and I completely overlooked a discrepancy in the dates. My assistant caught it, thank goodness, but I felt so embarrassed. I’m usually the one catching those things! And it’s because my mind is just... somewhere else. I’m looking at the document, but I’m actually worrying about my mother’s doctor appointment or the car or whatever else. 

And then... oh, this was just awful. We had this big presentation for a preservation grant we’re trying to secure. A really significant amount of money for the department. I had prepared the materials, I knew the data backwards and forwards, but when I stood up to present the budget breakdown to the committee... I just froze. I looked at the spreadsheet, and all I could think about was, "What if I calculated this wrong? What if we don't get the grant and it's my fault?" My heart started pounding, and I just lost my train of thought completely. I stood there for what felt like an eternity, and I actually had to hand off that part of the presentation to my co-director. I just stepped back and let him do it. 

That is so completely unlike me. I’ve been leading this team for six years, and I’ve never done anything like that. It was humiliating, to be honest. And now, of course, I’m worrying about *that*. I keep replaying it in my head, wondering what the committee thought, what my staff thinks of me now. I’m seriously starting to question whether I can even keep leading my team effectively if I can’t even get through a standard grant presentation without freezing up. It’s just... it feels like the worry is starting to seep into the one area of my life where I always felt totally in control, and I don't know how to stop it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, thank you for seeing me. I... I came in because, well, I just can't seem to stop worrying. It's like, once a worry starts, it just takes over completely. For example, my mother has been getting up a lot in the middle of the night lately, and I just lie awake thinking about her falling, or what if she leaves the stove on, even though she hasn't done that in years. And then, at work—I'm a librarian, and I handle a lot of our special collections and grant reports—if I have a report due, I obsess over every little detail. Did I miss a citation? Did I format the budget right? I make lists, you know, endless lists, trying to organize my thoughts and put them away, but I just can't seem to shelve the worries in my mind. I try to redirect myself, maybe read a book or watch a show, but the thoughts just keep looping and looping, no matter how hard I try to stop them. It's just exhausting, honestly, to constantly feel like I'm missing something or that something terrible is going to happen because I wasn't paying enough attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your worries?\n\nPatient: Rituals? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I know what you mean, like having to touch a doorknob a certain number of times or wash your hands over and over. I've read about things like that—we actually have a really robust psychology and behavioral sciences section in the library, and I process a lot of the new acquisitions, so I see those topics come across my desk quite a bit. But no, I don't do anything like that. I don't have a specific physical action or a routine I feel forced to do. \n\nIt's really more just... the lists I mentioned earlier. But I wouldn't call making those lists a ritual, exactly. It's more of a desperate attempt to just get the swirling thoughts out of my head and onto a piece of paper so maybe I can look at them objectively and organize them, the way I'd organize a catalog or a finding aid for an archive. But the thing is, it doesn't even work to reduce the worry! I'll write down \"check mother's medication schedule\" or \"verify the budget formatting on the humanities grant proposal,\" and instead of feeling relieved that it's written down and handled, I just stare at the paper and start worrying that I forgot to add something else to the list, or that I might accidentally throw the list away. \n\nSo, no, I don't have any behaviors that actually make the worry go away or that I feel compelled to perform in that strict sense. I honestly wish I did have something that worked to just turn it off. Sometimes I think it would be so much easier if I could just arrange the pens on my desk in a specific way and suddenly feel calm, but no, it doesn't work like that for me. It's strictly in my head. It's just this constant, endless mental loop of \"what if, what if, what if\" that I can't seem to stop with any sort of physical action, no matter how hard I try to just sit still and reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Um, intrusive thoughts? I guess it depends on what exactly you mean by intrusive. If you mean those really shocking or bizarre thoughts that just pop into your head out of nowhere—like the ones I have read about in some of the abnormal psychology journals we archive, where people imagine doing something terrible or completely out of character or just having these bizarre images flash in their minds—then no, I do not have anything like that at all. Nothing that feels alien or completely disconnected from who I am or what I care about. \n\nBut if you just mean thoughts that keep coming back and that I desperately want to go away, well, that really just goes back to the worries I was just talking about. They definitely recur. I mean, they are constantly recurring, all day long. And I absolutely try to ignore them or push them away. Like when I am trying to relax on a Sunday evening and suddenly I start obsessing over whether I properly secured the climate-controlled display case for the 18th-century manuscripts we just put out on the second floor. I try so hard to suppress it, I tell myself to just watch my television show and that the case is locked and the alarm is set, but the thought just keeps tapping me on the shoulder, over and over. \n\nBut it is not a weird or random thought, you see? It is directly related to my actual responsibilities as a librarian. It is just that the volume is turned up so high and it just will not leave me alone. So I do not think I have what you would call intrusive thoughts in that specific sense, just... very loud, very persistent everyday worries that I cannot seem to shut off no matter how much I try to distract myself or reason with my own brain.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a strong need to check things over and over, like your mother's medication schedule or the library's display cases?\n\nPatient: Well, check things over and over... no, not really. I mean, I check things, of course. As a librarian handling special collections, checking is just part of the job. When I set up a display case, I check the lock, I check the humidity monitor, I make sure the alarm sensor is active. That's just standard protocol. But I don't go back and check it five times, or feel some uncontrollable urge to keep testing the handle until my hand hurts, or anything like that. \n\nOnce I do it, I know I've done it. The physical act of checking is finished. It's just that later, when I'm at home, my mind starts telling me, \"Are you *sure* you checked it? What if you just thought you checked it, but you actually didn't turn the key all the way?\" So I'll sit there on my couch, agonizing over it, picturing the lock in my mind, trying to retrace my steps to prove to myself that I did it right. But I don't actually get up, drive back to the library, and physically check the case again. I just suffer through the worry in my head. \n\nAnd it's the same with my mother. I'll make sure her pill organizer is filled on Sunday afternoon. I do it carefully, I double-check the bottles against her chart, and I snap the little lids shut. I know it's done. But then on Wednesday night, I'll be lying in bed, wide awake, thinking, \"Did I put the blood pressure pill in the Tuesday slot or the Wednesday slot? What if she took two today? What if she misses a dose and has a stroke?\" I'll just lay there spinning out these awful scenarios. But I don't actually get out of bed, drive over to her house in the middle of the night, and count her pills. I just... worry about it. Endlessly. So the checking isn't the problem, it's the worrying about whether my checking was good enough that just completely exhausts me.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about everyday things that could potentially happen to you or someone you care about?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes, exactly. They are very much about everyday things, but it’s like... it’s like they just take over everything. It used to be mostly just about my mother, you know? Just worrying if she was safe, if her health was okay, if she was taking her medication like I mentioned. That was always a background hum. But over this past year, it’s like the worry has just... spread. It’s metastasized, for lack of a better word. \n\nNow it’s not just her. It’s my finances—I’ll sit there wondering if I’m putting enough into my retirement account, or if the roof on the house is going to need replacing soon and how I’ll afford it. And then it bleeds into my work at the library. I’ll agonize over staff decisions, like if I scheduled the student workers properly or if I was too harsh when I corrected someone about the cataloging system. And the deadlines! We have these grant deadlines for preservation projects, and even if we are weeks ahead of schedule, I just feel this knot in my stomach that we’re going to miss it, that the funding will fall through, and it will be entirely my fault. \n\nAnd then it comes back to the house, like I said, or just... am I being a good daughter? Am I doing enough for my mother, or am I just going through the motions? It’s exhausting. Even the smallest things, like a slightly weird noise from my car engine on the way to work, or an email from the library director that just says \"see me later\"—things that most people would just brush off—they suddenly feel like this massive thread that, if I pull it, my whole life is just going to completely unravel. So, yes, they are everyday things, absolutely, but the sheer volume of them, and how heavy they feel... it’s just overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries interfering with your ability to perform your job as a librarian or affecting your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... yes, they really are. They are very much about everyday things, but it’s like... it’s like they just take over everything. It used to be mostly just about my mother, you know? Just worrying if she was safe, if her health was okay, if she was taking her medication like I mentioned. That was always a background hum. But over this past year, it’s like the worry has just... spread. It’s metastasized, for lack of a better word. \n\nNow it’s not just her. It’s my finances—I’ll sit there wondering if I’m putting enough into my retirement account, or if the roof on the house is going to need replacing soon and how I’ll afford it. And then it bleeds into my work at the library. I’ll agonize over staff decisions, like if I scheduled the student workers properly or if I was too harsh when I corrected someone about the cataloging system. And the deadlines! We have these grant deadlines for preservation projects, and even if we are weeks ahead of schedule, I just feel this knot in my stomach that we’re going to miss it, that the funding will fall through, and it will be entirely my fault. \n\nAnd then it comes back to the house, like I said, or just... am I being a good daughter? Am I doing enough for my mother, or am I just going through the motions? It’s exhausting. Even the smallest things, like a slightly weird noise from my car engine on the way to work, or an email from the library director that just says \"see me later\"—things that most people would just brush off—they suddenly feel like this massive thread that, if I pull it, my whole life is just going to completely unravel. So, yes, they are everyday things, absolutely, but the sheer volume of them, and how heavy they feel... it’s just overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries interfering with your ability to perform your job as a librarian or affecting your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... yes, they really are. And that’s honestly one of the things that’s scaring me the most right now, because I have always prided myself on being very competent, very detail-oriented. You have to be, in special collections. But lately... well, I’ve started missing things. Small things, mostly, but important details. Like, just last week, I was reviewing the provenance records for a new acquisition, and I completely overlooked a discrepancy in the dates. My assistant caught it, thank goodness, but I felt so embarrassed. I’m usually the one catching those things! And it’s because my mind is just... somewhere else. I’m looking at the document, but I’m actually worrying about my mother’s doctor appointment or the car or whatever else. \n\nAnd then... oh, this was just awful. We had this big presentation for a preservation grant we’re trying to secure. A really significant amount of money for the department. I had prepared the materials, I knew the data backwards and forwards, but when I stood up to present the budget breakdown to the committee... I just froze. I looked at the spreadsheet, and all I could think about was, \"What if I calculated this wrong? What if we don't get the grant and it's my fault?\" My heart started pounding, and I just lost my train of thought completely. I stood there for what felt like an eternity, and I actually had to hand off that part of the presentation to my co-director. I just stepped back and let him do it. \n\nThat is so completely unlike me. I’ve been leading this team for six years, and I’ve never done anything like that. It was humiliating, to be honest. And now, of course, I’m worrying about *that*. I keep replaying it in my head, wondering what the committee thought, what my staff thinks of me now. I’m seriously starting to question whether I can even keep leading my team effectively if I can’t even get through a standard grant presentation without freezing up. It’s just... it feels like the worry is starting to seep into the one area of my life where I always felt totally in control, and I don't know how to stop it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself avoiding certain situations or tasks because of your worries?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

Avoid? No... no, I don't think so. I mean, I still do everything. Actually, it's almost the opposite, I feel this intense pressure that I *must* do them, and I have to do them right. Like that grant presentation I was just telling you about—I didn't call in sick, you know? I didn't try to pass it off to my co-director beforehand, even though I had been dreading it for weeks and losing sleep over it. I was there, in my suit, standing right at the podium. I just... malfunctioned once I was up there. But I certainly didn't avoid going. 

And with my mother... I mean, I suppose I could theoretically hire a home health aide to organize her pills or take her to the clinic so I wouldn't have to carry that burden and worry about making a mistake, but I don't. I still go over to her house every single Sunday at 2 PM sharp. I sit down at her kitchen table—it’s this heavy old oak table she’s had since I was a little girl, the kind with the big claw feet—and I lay out every single one of her prescription bottles. I feel that horrible knot in my stomach the entire time, my chest gets tight just thinking, 'what if I mess this up and something terrible happens to her,' but I don't walk away. I sit there, I sort the pills, I snap the little lids shut. I push through it. 

It's the exact same at the library. Even when I'm absolutely terrified about the preservation deadlines or the budget, I don't procrastinate or hide in my office. If anything, I overcompensate. I'm usually weeks ahead of schedule. I'll be at my desk at 7:30 in the morning, before anyone else even gets to the building, before the heat even really kicks on in the offices, pulling up the spreadsheets and just staring at the numbers, agonizing over them. 

So, no, I wouldn't say I avoid things. I just... I endure them, I guess you could say. I drag all this worry with me into every single task, like this incredibly heavy suitcase I can't ever put down, but I still show up. I still do the work. I just suffer the whole time I'm doing it. I just don't have it in my nature to run away from my responsibilities, even when they make me feel completely miserable. Does that make sense? I just push through, no matter how bad the worry gets.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, thank you for seeing me. I... I came in because, well, I just can't seem to stop worrying. It's like, once a worry starts, it just takes over completely. For example, my mother has been getting up a lot in the middle of the night lately, and I just lie awake thinking about her falling, or what if she leaves the stove on, even though she hasn't done that in years. And then, at work—I'm a librarian, and I handle a lot of our special collections and grant reports—if I have a report due, I obsess over every little detail. Did I miss a citation? Did I format the budget right? I make lists, you know, endless lists, trying to organize my thoughts and put them away, but I just can't seem to shelve the worries in my mind. I try to redirect myself, maybe read a book or watch a show, but the thoughts just keep looping and looping, no matter how hard I try to stop them. It's just exhausting, honestly, to constantly feel like I'm missing something or that something terrible is going to happen because I wasn't paying enough attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your worries?\n\nPatient: Rituals? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I know what you mean, like having to touch a doorknob a certain number of times or wash your hands over and over. I've read about things like that—we actually have a really robust psychology and behavioral sciences section in the library, and I process a lot of the new acquisitions, so I see those topics come across my desk quite a bit. But no, I don't do anything like that. I don't have a specific physical action or a routine I feel forced to do. \n\nIt's really more just... the lists I mentioned earlier. But I wouldn't call making those lists a ritual, exactly. It's more of a desperate attempt to just get the swirling thoughts out of my head and onto a piece of paper so maybe I can look at them objectively and organize them, the way I'd organize a catalog or a finding aid for an archive. But the thing is, it doesn't even work to reduce the worry! I'll write down \"check mother's medication schedule\" or \"verify the budget formatting on the humanities grant proposal,\" and instead of feeling relieved that it's written down and handled, I just stare at the paper and start worrying that I forgot to add something else to the list, or that I might accidentally throw the list away. \n\nSo, no, I don't have any behaviors that actually make the worry go away or that I feel compelled to perform in that strict sense. I honestly wish I did have something that worked to just turn it off. Sometimes I think it would be so much easier if I could just arrange the pens on my desk in a specific way and suddenly feel calm, but no, it doesn't work like that for me. It's strictly in my head. It's just this constant, endless mental loop of \"what if, what if, what if\" that I can't seem to stop with any sort of physical action, no matter how hard I try to just sit still and reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Um, intrusive thoughts? I guess it depends on what exactly you mean by intrusive. If you mean those really shocking or bizarre thoughts that just pop into your head out of nowhere—like the ones I have read about in some of the abnormal psychology journals we archive, where people imagine doing something terrible or completely out of character or just having these bizarre images flash in their minds—then no, I do not have anything like that at all. Nothing that feels alien or completely disconnected from who I am or what I care about. \n\nBut if you just mean thoughts that keep coming back and that I desperately want to go away, well, that really just goes back to the worries I was just talking about. They definitely recur. I mean, they are constantly recurring, all day long. And I absolutely try to ignore them or push them away. Like when I am trying to relax on a Sunday evening and suddenly I start obsessing over whether I properly secured the climate-controlled display case for the 18th-century manuscripts we just put out on the second floor. I try so hard to suppress it, I tell myself to just watch my television show and that the case is locked and the alarm is set, but the thought just keeps tapping me on the shoulder, over and over. \n\nBut it is not a weird or random thought, you see? It is directly related to my actual responsibilities as a librarian. It is just that the volume is turned up so high and it just will not leave me alone. So I do not think I have what you would call intrusive thoughts in that specific sense, just... very loud, very persistent everyday worries that I cannot seem to shut off no matter how much I try to distract myself or reason with my own brain.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a strong need to check things over and over, like your mother's medication schedule or the library's display cases?\n\nPatient: Well, check things over and over... no, not really. I mean, I check things, of course. As a librarian handling special collections, checking is just part of the job. When I set up a display case, I check the lock, I check the humidity monitor, I make sure the alarm sensor is active. That's just standard protocol. But I don't go back and check it five times, or feel some uncontrollable urge to keep testing the handle until my hand hurts, or anything like that. \n\nOnce I do it, I know I've done it. The physical act of checking is finished. It's just that later, when I'm at home, my mind starts telling me, \"Are you *sure* you checked it? What if you just thought you checked it, but you actually didn't turn the key all the way?\" So I'll sit there on my couch, agonizing over it, picturing the lock in my mind, trying to retrace my steps to prove to myself that I did it right. But I don't actually get up, drive back to the library, and physically check the case again. I just suffer through the worry in my head. \n\nAnd it's the same with my mother. I'll make sure her pill organizer is filled on Sunday afternoon. I do it carefully, I double-check the bottles against her chart, and I snap the little lids shut. I know it's done. But then on Wednesday night, I'll be lying in bed, wide awake, thinking, \"Did I put the blood pressure pill in the Tuesday slot or the Wednesday slot? What if she took two today? What if she misses a dose and has a stroke?\" I'll just lay there spinning out these awful scenarios. But I don't actually get out of bed, drive over to her house in the middle of the night, and count her pills. I just... worry about it. Endlessly. So the checking isn't the problem, it's the worrying about whether my checking was good enough that just completely exhausts me.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about everyday things that could potentially happen to you or someone you care about?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes, exactly. They are very much about everyday things, but it’s like... it’s like they just take over everything. It used to be mostly just about my mother, you know? Just worrying if she was safe, if her health was okay, if she was taking her medication like I mentioned. That was always a background hum. But over this past year, it’s like the worry has just... spread. It’s metastasized, for lack of a better word. \n\nNow it’s not just her. It’s my finances—I’ll sit there wondering if I’m putting enough into my retirement account, or if the roof on the house is going to need replacing soon and how I’ll afford it. And then it bleeds into my work at the library. I’ll agonize over staff decisions, like if I scheduled the student workers properly or if I was too harsh when I corrected someone about the cataloging system. And the deadlines! We have these grant deadlines for preservation projects, and even if we are weeks ahead of schedule, I just feel this knot in my stomach that we’re going to miss it, that the funding will fall through, and it will be entirely my fault. \n\nAnd then it comes back to the house, like I said, or just... am I being a good daughter? Am I doing enough for my mother, or am I just going through the motions? It’s exhausting. Even the smallest things, like a slightly weird noise from my car engine on the way to work, or an email from the library director that just says \"see me later\"—things that most people would just brush off—they suddenly feel like this massive thread that, if I pull it, my whole life is just going to completely unravel. So, yes, they are everyday things, absolutely, but the sheer volume of them, and how heavy they feel... it’s just overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries interfering with your ability to perform your job as a librarian or affecting your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... yes, they really are. And that’s honestly one of the things that’s scaring me the most right now, because I have always prided myself on being very competent, very detail-oriented. You have to be, in special collections. But lately... well, I’ve started missing things. Small things, mostly, but important details. Like, just last week, I was reviewing the provenance records for a new acquisition, and I completely overlooked a discrepancy in the dates. My assistant caught it, thank goodness, but I felt so embarrassed. I’m usually the one catching those things! And it’s because my mind is just... somewhere else. I’m looking at the document, but I’m actually worrying about my mother’s doctor appointment or the car or whatever else. \n\nAnd then... oh, this was just awful. We had this big presentation for a preservation grant we’re trying to secure. A really significant amount of money for the department. I had prepared the materials, I knew the data backwards and forwards, but when I stood up to present the budget breakdown to the committee... I just froze. I looked at the spreadsheet, and all I could think about was, \"What if I calculated this wrong? What if we don't get the grant and it's my fault?\" My heart started pounding, and I just lost my train of thought completely. I stood there for what felt like an eternity, and I actually had to hand off that part of the presentation to my co-director. I just stepped back and let him do it. \n\nThat is so completely unlike me. I’ve been leading this team for six years, and I’ve never done anything like that. It was humiliating, to be honest. And now, of course, I’m worrying about *that*. I keep replaying it in my head, wondering what the committee thought, what my staff thinks of me now. I’m seriously starting to question whether I can even keep leading my team effectively if I can’t even get through a standard grant presentation without freezing up. It’s just... it feels like the worry is starting to seep into the one area of my life where I always felt totally in control, and I don't know how to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or tasks because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Avoid? No... no, I don't think so. I mean, I still do everything. Actually, it's almost the opposite, I feel this intense pressure that I *must* do them, and I have to do them right. Like that grant presentation I was just telling you about—I didn't call in sick, you know? I didn't try to pass it off to my co-director beforehand, even though I had been dreading it for weeks and losing sleep over it. I was there, in my suit, standing right at the podium. I just... malfunctioned once I was up there. But I certainly didn't avoid going. \n\nAnd with my mother... I mean, I suppose I could theoretically hire a home health aide to organize her pills or take her to the clinic so I wouldn't have to carry that burden and worry about making a mistake, but I don't. I still go over to her house every single Sunday at 2 PM sharp. I sit down at her kitchen table—it’s this heavy old oak table she’s had since I was a little girl, the kind with the big claw feet—and I lay out every single one of her prescription bottles. I feel that horrible knot in my stomach the entire time, my chest gets tight just thinking, 'what if I mess this up and something terrible happens to her,' but I don't walk away. I sit there, I sort the pills, I snap the little lids shut. I push through it. \n\nIt's the exact same at the library. Even when I'm absolutely terrified about the preservation deadlines or the budget, I don't procrastinate or hide in my office. If anything, I overcompensate. I'm usually weeks ahead of schedule. I'll be at my desk at 7:30 in the morning, before anyone else even gets to the building, before the heat even really kicks on in the offices, pulling up the spreadsheets and just staring at the numbers, agonizing over them. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I avoid things. I just... I endure them, I guess you could say. I drag all this worry with me into every single task, like this incredibly heavy suitcase I can't ever put down, but I still show up. I still do the work. I just suffer the whole time I'm doing it. I just don't have it in my nature to run away from my responsibilities, even when they make me feel completely miserable. Does that make sense? I just push through, no matter how bad the worry gets.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I just push through, no matter how bad the worry gets.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your worries are causing you a lot of distress?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / librarian_or_media_collections_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Distress? Oh, yes. Yes, absolutely. I don't think "distress" even quite captures it, honestly. It’s... it’s this constant, suffocating weight. I mean, I wake up with it, I go through my entire day with it, and I take it to bed with me. It’s just always there. I'll be sitting in my office in the special collections wing—and it's usually so peaceful down there, you know, we keep the environment strictly regulated for the rare books and manuscripts, so it's always this cool, perfectly quiet, still space—but inside my head, it is just absolute chaos. Just a hurricane of "what ifs." 

It takes the joy out of absolutely everything. I used to really love my Sundays with my mother. We’d have tea, we’d talk about the neighborhood, I'd look forward to it all week... but now, the whole time I’m there, I’m just so distressed about her health, about the pills, about whether I’m doing enough for her. And it’s the exact same at work. I used to feel this deep, genuine sense of satisfaction when we'd secure a grant or finish archiving a difficult collection, and now I just feel... I don't know, maybe a temporary, tiny sliver of relief before the next worry immediately starts up. 

It’s just so emotionally draining to live like this. I feel like I'm running a marathon every single day, but I haven't even left my desk. I just feel so worn down by it all, like my brain is an engine that's been running in the red for months and it’s just burning out. So, yes, it is incredibly distressing. I just want a break from my own mind, you know? Just one single day where I don't feel like the sky is about to fall on me or the people I care about. It’s just... it’s just too much.

---------- turn 8 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F42\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, thank you for seeing me. I... I came in because, well, I just can't seem to stop worrying. It's like, once a worry starts, it just takes over completely. For example, my mother has been getting up a lot in the middle of the night lately, and I just lie awake thinking about her falling, or what if she leaves the stove on, even though she hasn't done that in years. And then, at work—I'm a librarian, and I handle a lot of our special collections and grant reports—if I have a report due, I obsess over every little detail. Did I miss a citation? Did I format the budget right? I make lists, you know, endless lists, trying to organize my thoughts and put them away, but I just can't seem to shelve the worries in my mind. I try to redirect myself, maybe read a book or watch a show, but the thoughts just keep looping and looping, no matter how hard I try to stop them. It's just exhausting, honestly, to constantly feel like I'm missing something or that something terrible is going to happen because I wasn't paying enough attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your worries?\n\nPatient: Rituals? Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I know what you mean, like having to touch a doorknob a certain number of times or wash your hands over and over. I've read about things like that—we actually have a really robust psychology and behavioral sciences section in the library, and I process a lot of the new acquisitions, so I see those topics come across my desk quite a bit. But no, I don't do anything like that. I don't have a specific physical action or a routine I feel forced to do. \n\nIt's really more just... the lists I mentioned earlier. But I wouldn't call making those lists a ritual, exactly. It's more of a desperate attempt to just get the swirling thoughts out of my head and onto a piece of paper so maybe I can look at them objectively and organize them, the way I'd organize a catalog or a finding aid for an archive. But the thing is, it doesn't even work to reduce the worry! I'll write down \"check mother's medication schedule\" or \"verify the budget formatting on the humanities grant proposal,\" and instead of feeling relieved that it's written down and handled, I just stare at the paper and start worrying that I forgot to add something else to the list, or that I might accidentally throw the list away. \n\nSo, no, I don't have any behaviors that actually make the worry go away or that I feel compelled to perform in that strict sense. I honestly wish I did have something that worked to just turn it off. Sometimes I think it would be so much easier if I could just arrange the pens on my desk in a specific way and suddenly feel calm, but no, it doesn't work like that for me. It's strictly in my head. It's just this constant, endless mental loop of \"what if, what if, what if\" that I can't seem to stop with any sort of physical action, no matter how hard I try to just sit still and reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Um, intrusive thoughts? I guess it depends on what exactly you mean by intrusive. If you mean those really shocking or bizarre thoughts that just pop into your head out of nowhere—like the ones I have read about in some of the abnormal psychology journals we archive, where people imagine doing something terrible or completely out of character or just having these bizarre images flash in their minds—then no, I do not have anything like that at all. Nothing that feels alien or completely disconnected from who I am or what I care about. \n\nBut if you just mean thoughts that keep coming back and that I desperately want to go away, well, that really just goes back to the worries I was just talking about. They definitely recur. I mean, they are constantly recurring, all day long. And I absolutely try to ignore them or push them away. Like when I am trying to relax on a Sunday evening and suddenly I start obsessing over whether I properly secured the climate-controlled display case for the 18th-century manuscripts we just put out on the second floor. I try so hard to suppress it, I tell myself to just watch my television show and that the case is locked and the alarm is set, but the thought just keeps tapping me on the shoulder, over and over. \n\nBut it is not a weird or random thought, you see? It is directly related to my actual responsibilities as a librarian. It is just that the volume is turned up so high and it just will not leave me alone. So I do not think I have what you would call intrusive thoughts in that specific sense, just... very loud, very persistent everyday worries that I cannot seem to shut off no matter how much I try to distract myself or reason with my own brain.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a strong need to check things over and over, like your mother's medication schedule or the library's display cases?\n\nPatient: Well, check things over and over... no, not really. I mean, I check things, of course. As a librarian handling special collections, checking is just part of the job. When I set up a display case, I check the lock, I check the humidity monitor, I make sure the alarm sensor is active. That's just standard protocol. But I don't go back and check it five times, or feel some uncontrollable urge to keep testing the handle until my hand hurts, or anything like that. \n\nOnce I do it, I know I've done it. The physical act of checking is finished. It's just that later, when I'm at home, my mind starts telling me, \"Are you *sure* you checked it? What if you just thought you checked it, but you actually didn't turn the key all the way?\" So I'll sit there on my couch, agonizing over it, picturing the lock in my mind, trying to retrace my steps to prove to myself that I did it right. But I don't actually get up, drive back to the library, and physically check the case again. I just suffer through the worry in my head. \n\nAnd it's the same with my mother. I'll make sure her pill organizer is filled on Sunday afternoon. I do it carefully, I double-check the bottles against her chart, and I snap the little lids shut. I know it's done. But then on Wednesday night, I'll be lying in bed, wide awake, thinking, \"Did I put the blood pressure pill in the Tuesday slot or the Wednesday slot? What if she took two today? What if she misses a dose and has a stroke?\" I'll just lay there spinning out these awful scenarios. But I don't actually get out of bed, drive over to her house in the middle of the night, and count her pills. I just... worry about it. Endlessly. So the checking isn't the problem, it's the worrying about whether my checking was good enough that just completely exhausts me.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about everyday things that could potentially happen to you or someone you care about?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes, exactly. They are very much about everyday things, but it’s like... it’s like they just take over everything. It used to be mostly just about my mother, you know? Just worrying if she was safe, if her health was okay, if she was taking her medication like I mentioned. That was always a background hum. But over this past year, it’s like the worry has just... spread. It’s metastasized, for lack of a better word. \n\nNow it’s not just her. It’s my finances—I’ll sit there wondering if I’m putting enough into my retirement account, or if the roof on the house is going to need replacing soon and how I’ll afford it. And then it bleeds into my work at the library. I’ll agonize over staff decisions, like if I scheduled the student workers properly or if I was too harsh when I corrected someone about the cataloging system. And the deadlines! We have these grant deadlines for preservation projects, and even if we are weeks ahead of schedule, I just feel this knot in my stomach that we’re going to miss it, that the funding will fall through, and it will be entirely my fault. \n\nAnd then it comes back to the house, like I said, or just... am I being a good daughter? Am I doing enough for my mother, or am I just going through the motions? It’s exhausting. Even the smallest things, like a slightly weird noise from my car engine on the way to work, or an email from the library director that just says \"see me later\"—things that most people would just brush off—they suddenly feel like this massive thread that, if I pull it, my whole life is just going to completely unravel. So, yes, they are everyday things, absolutely, but the sheer volume of them, and how heavy they feel... it’s just overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries interfering with your ability to perform your job as a librarian or affecting your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... yes, they really are. And that’s honestly one of the things that’s scaring me the most right now, because I have always prided myself on being very competent, very detail-oriented. You have to be, in special collections. But lately... well, I’ve started missing things. Small things, mostly, but important details. Like, just last week, I was reviewing the provenance records for a new acquisition, and I completely overlooked a discrepancy in the dates. My assistant caught it, thank goodness, but I felt so embarrassed. I’m usually the one catching those things! And it’s because my mind is just... somewhere else. I’m looking at the document, but I’m actually worrying about my mother’s doctor appointment or the car or whatever else. \n\nAnd then... oh, this was just awful. We had this big presentation for a preservation grant we’re trying to secure. A really significant amount of money for the department. I had prepared the materials, I knew the data backwards and forwards, but when I stood up to present the budget breakdown to the committee... I just froze. I looked at the spreadsheet, and all I could think about was, \"What if I calculated this wrong? What if we don't get the grant and it's my fault?\" My heart started pounding, and I just lost my train of thought completely. I stood there for what felt like an eternity, and I actually had to hand off that part of the presentation to my co-director. I just stepped back and let him do it. \n\nThat is so completely unlike me. I’ve been leading this team for six years, and I’ve never done anything like that. It was humiliating, to be honest. And now, of course, I’m worrying about *that*. I keep replaying it in my head, wondering what the committee thought, what my staff thinks of me now. I’m seriously starting to question whether I can even keep leading my team effectively if I can’t even get through a standard grant presentation without freezing up. It’s just... it feels like the worry is starting to seep into the one area of my life where I always felt totally in control, and I don't know how to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or tasks because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Avoid? No... no, I don't think so. I mean, I still do everything. Actually, it's almost the opposite, I feel this intense pressure that I *must* do them, and I have to do them right. Like that grant presentation I was just telling you about—I didn't call in sick, you know? I didn't try to pass it off to my co-director beforehand, even though I had been dreading it for weeks and losing sleep over it. I was there, in my suit, standing right at the podium. I just... malfunctioned once I was up there. But I certainly didn't avoid going. \n\nAnd with my mother... I mean, I suppose I could theoretically hire a home health aide to organize her pills or take her to the clinic so I wouldn't have to carry that burden and worry about making a mistake, but I don't. I still go over to her house every single Sunday at 2 PM sharp. I sit down at her kitchen table—it’s this heavy old oak table she’s had since I was a little girl, the kind with the big claw feet—and I lay out every single one of her prescription bottles. I feel that horrible knot in my stomach the entire time, my chest gets tight just thinking, 'what if I mess this up and something terrible happens to her,' but I don't walk away. I sit there, I sort the pills, I snap the little lids shut. I push through it. \n\nIt's the exact same at the library. Even when I'm absolutely terrified about the preservation deadlines or the budget, I don't procrastinate or hide in my office. If anything, I overcompensate. I'm usually weeks ahead of schedule. I'll be at my desk at 7:30 in the morning, before anyone else even gets to the building, before the heat even really kicks on in the offices, pulling up the spreadsheets and just staring at the numbers, agonizing over them. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I avoid things. I just... I endure them, I guess you could say. I drag all this worry with me into every single task, like this incredibly heavy suitcase I can't ever put down, but I still show up. I still do the work. I just suffer the whole time I'm doing it. I just don't have it in my nature to run away from my responsibilities, even when they make me feel completely miserable. Does that make sense? I just push through, no matter how bad the worry gets.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Distress? Oh, yes. Yes, absolutely. I don't think \"distress\" even quite captures it, honestly. It’s... it’s this constant, suffocating weight. I mean, I wake up with it, I go through my entire day with it, and I take it to bed with me. It’s just always there. I'll be sitting in my office in the special collections wing—and it's usually so peaceful down there, you know, we keep the environment strictly regulated for the rare books and manuscripts, so it's always this cool, perfectly quiet, still space—but inside my head, it is just absolute chaos. Just a hurricane of \"what ifs.\" \n\nIt takes the joy out of absolutely everything. I used to really love my Sundays with my mother. We’d have tea, we’d talk about the neighborhood, I'd look forward to it all week... but now, the whole time I’m there, I’m just so distressed about her health, about the pills, about whether I’m doing enough for her. And it’s the exact same at work. I used to feel this deep, genuine sense of satisfaction when we'd secure a grant or finish archiving a difficult collection, and now I just feel... I don't know, maybe a temporary, tiny sliver of relief before the next worry immediately starts up. \n\nIt’s just so emotionally draining to live like this. I feel like I'm running a marathon every single day, but I haven't even left my desk. I just feel so worn down by it all, like my brain is an engine that's been running in the red for months and it’s just burning out. So, yes, it is incredibly distressing. I just want a break from my own mind, you know? Just one single day where I don't feel like the sky is about to fall on me or the people I care about. It’s just... it’s just too much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I didn't try to pass it off to my co-director beforehand, even though I had been dreading it for weeks and losing sleep over it. I was there, in my suit, standing right at the podium. I just... malfunctioned once I was up there. But I certainly didn't avoid going. \n\nAnd with my mother... I mean, I suppose I could theoretically hire a home health aide to organize her pills or take her to the clinic so I wouldn't have to carry that burden and worry about making a mistake, but I don't. I still go over to her house every single Sunday at 2 PM sharp. I sit down at her kitchen table—it’s this heavy old oak table she’s had since I was a little girl, the kind with the big claw feet—and I lay out every single one of her prescription bottles. I feel that horrible knot in my stomach the entire time, my chest gets tight just thinking, 'what if I mess this up and something terrible happens to her,' but I don't walk away. I sit there, I sort the pills, I snap the little lids shut. I push through it. \n\nIt's the exact same at the library. Even when I'm absolutely terrified about the preservation deadlines or the budget, I don't procrastinate or hide in my office. If anything, I overcompensate. I'm usually weeks ahead of schedule. I'll be at my desk at 7:30 in the morning, before anyone else even gets to the building, before the heat even really kicks on in the offices, pulling up the spreadsheets and just staring at the numbers, agonizing over them. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I avoid things. I just... I endure them, I guess you could say. I drag all this worry with me into every single task, like this incredibly heavy suitcase I can't ever put down, but I still show up. I still do the work. I just suffer the whole time I'm doing it. I just don't have it in my nature to run away from my responsibilities, even when they make me feel completely miserable. Does that make sense? I just push through, no matter how bad the worry gets.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Distress? Oh, yes. Yes, absolutely. I don't think \"distress\" even quite captures it, honestly. It’s... it’s this constant, suffocating weight. I mean, I wake up with it, I go through my entire day with it, and I take it to bed with me. It’s just always there. I'll be sitting in my office in the special collections wing—and it's usually so peaceful down there, you know, we keep the environment strictly regulated for the rare books and manuscripts, so it's always this cool, perfectly quiet, still space—but inside my head, it is just absolute chaos. Just a hurricane of \"what ifs.\" \n\nIt takes the joy out of absolutely everything. I used to really love my Sundays with my mother. We’d have tea, we’d talk about the neighborhood, I'd look forward to it all week... but now, the whole time I’m there, I’m just so distressed about her health, about the pills, about whether I’m doing enough for her. And it’s the exact same at work. I used to feel this deep, genuine sense of satisfaction when we'd secure a grant or finish archiving a difficult collection, and now I just feel... I don't know, maybe a temporary, tiny sliver of relief before the next worry immediately starts up. \n\nIt’s just so emotionally draining to live like this. I feel like I'm running a marathon every single day, but I haven't even left my desk. I just feel so worn down by it all, like my brain is an engine that's been running in the red for months and it’s just burning out. So, yes, it is incredibly distressing. I just want a break from my own mind, you know? Just one single day where I don't feel like the sky is about to fall on me or the people I care about. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes... I mean, they aren't completely made up scenarios, if that's what you mean. They are real things. But it's... it's the sheer volume of it now. Over this past year, my worrying has just spread so much. It used to really just be focused on my mother’s safety, which, you know, makes sense because she's getting older and living alone with those heavy oak stairs in her house. But now? Now it's just attached itself to absolutely everything. 

I worry constantly about our finances, looking at the accounts and agonizing over whether we're saving enough or if one emergency is going to bankrupt us. And work... I'm losing sleep over routine staff decisions, like who to assign to which archiving project, and the deadlines for those projects, which I used to manage in my sleep! And the house, oh goodness. Just the other day I noticed a tiny little water spot near the guest room window and I immediately spiraled into thinking the whole roof was rotting and we'd need tens of thousands of dollars to fix it. And on top of all that, I have this constant, heavy guilt weighing on me about whether I’m being a good daughter, even though I'm there every single Sunday sorting those pills just like I told you. 

It’s like... I don't know, it's like even the small things can start to feel like they’re about to completely unravel. Like pulling a loose thread on a sweater and suddenly the whole sleeve is coming apart in your hands. I know logically that a water spot might just need a bit of caulk, and my staff is perfectly capable of meeting their deadlines, but my brain just won't accept the logical answer anymore. It just latches onto the worst-case scenario for every single daily task. So yes, they are realistic things to think about, but the way I'm worrying about them... it's just taken over every corner of my life this past year, and I just can't seem to rein it back in to a normal level.
